documentation†help.devpost.com】\nL67: \nL68:   *   * 【1†Log in†secure.devpost.com】\nL69:   * 【2†Sign up†secure.devpost.com】\nL70: # CUTLINE: Evidence-Aware Documentary Review\nL71: \nL72: CUTLINE is a Gemini-powered documentary review agent that uses Parallel Search to verify narration, propose factual repairs, preserve human approval, and export audit-ready edits.\nL73: \nL74: 【19†Like†secure.devpost.com】\nL75: \nL76: 【20†Comment】\nL77: \nL78:   * #### 【21†Story】\nL79: \nL80:   * #### 【20†Updates】\nL81: \nL82:   * #### 【22†Submission history】\nL83: \nL84:   * 【23†iframe†www.youtube.com】\nL85: \nL86:   * Handoff tab of Cutline\nL87: \nL88:   * Impact tab of Cutline\nL89: \nL90:   * Landing Page of Cutline\nL91: \nL92:   * Script tab of Cutline\nL93:   * Evidence tab of Cutline\nL94: ## Inspiration\nL95: \nL96: Documentary teams make hundreds of editorial decisions while scripts, narration, and cuts continue to change. Fact-checking is often fragmented across browser tabs, spreadsheets, chat threads, and review notes. That creates two risks: unsupported claims can survive until late in production, and evidence collected for an earlier version of a sentence can be mistaken for evidence supporting the final cut.\nL97: We built CUTLINE to make documentary research part of the editorial workflow itself. The goal is not to let AI publish facts autonomously. It is to help researchers and editors find evidence faster while preserving revision history, source traceability, and human sign-off.\nL98: ## What it does\nL99: \nL100: CUTLINE is an evidence-aware review workspace for documentary narration.\nL101: \nL102: An editor can:\nL103:   1. Select a narration cue containing a factual claim.\nL104:   2. Ask CUTLINE to research that exact wording.\nL105:   3. Review public sources retrieved through Parallel Search.\nL106:   4. See whether Gemini assesses the claim as supported, contradicted, or unresolved.\nL107:   5. Apply a proposed factual repair without changing quoted source material.\nL108:   6. Re-run research whenever the wording changes.\nL109:   7. Complete human review and sign-off.\nL110:   8. Export the approved result as JSON, CSV, or text.\nL111: CUTLINE treats evidence as revision-specific. If an editor changes a cue after research or approval, dependent evidence and review state become stale. Export remains blocked until the corrected revision has current evidence and approval.\nL112: ## How we built it\nL113: \nL114: The public web application runs on Google Cloud Run with a FastAPI backend and a focused HTML, CSS, and JavaScript interface.\nL115: \nL116: The production workflow uses:\nL117:   * Gemini on Google Cloud for evidence-aware assessment and repair suggestions.\nL118:   * Parallel Search API for live retrieval of public documentary evidence.\nL119:   * Google ADK and Agent Engine for the deployed claim-research agent.\nL120:   * Cloud Firestore for revisioned project persistence and optimistic concurrency.\nL121:   * Secret Manager for runtime provider credentials.\nL122:   * Cloud IAM for least-privilege service identities.\nL123:   * Cloud Build and Artifact Registry for reproducible container deployment.\nL124:   * Cloud Logging for sanitized operational diagnostics.\nL125: The browser never receives the Parallel credential. The Cloud Run application and Agent Engine runtime obtain access through separate service identities, and the Agent Engine identity receives access only to the required secret.\nL126: ## How Parallel powers the workflow\nL127: \nL128: Parallel is an active runtime dependency, not a label or static dataset.\nL129: \nL130: The `search_documentary_evidence` agent tool calls Parallel Search with the current claim, normalizes the returned records, validates public URLs, and passes structured evidence to Gemini. CUTLINE stores source IDs separately from claim assessments so editors can inspect exactly which records support a result.\nL131: In our release validation, the remotely deployed Agent Engine created a managed session, invoked the Parallel tool, returned five public source records from domains including NASA, the Smithsonian, and the Lunar and Planetary Institute, and produced a final Gemini response.\nL132: ## Challenges we faced\nL133: \nL134: Production deployment exposed several real-world issues beyond the local prototype.\nL135: \nL136: Cloud Run initially rejected its startup probe because framework host validation did not recognize the probe host. We implemented a narrowly scoped health-probe middleware rather than weakening host validation for the entire application.\nL137: \nL138: We also diagnosed a rotated Parallel credential, pinned the valid secret version, and verified both direct provider access and the complete application workflow.\nL139: Agent Engine required additional work around SDK packaging, delayed service-agent provisioning, package-relative imports, Cloud Resource Manager availability, and model endpoint compatibility. We corrected those issues while preserving the existing resource and preventing accidental duplicate deployments.\nL140: ## Accomplishments\nL141: \nL142: We are proud that CUTLINE is more than a prompt wrapped in a form.\nL143: \nL144: It includes:\nL145:   * A complete research, repair, re-research, review, sign-off, and export workflow.\nL146:   * Live Parallel and Gemini calls.\nL147:   * Revision-aware evidence invalidation.\nL148:   * Immutable source quotations.\nL149:   * Human-controlled approval.\nL150:   * Firestore persistence across Cloud Run revisions.\nL151:   * Explicit deletion and TTL-based cleanup.\nL152:   * CSRF protection, owner isolation, secure cookies, security headers, and validated public URLs.\nL153:   * A remotely deployed Google ADK Agent Engine with a verified Parallel tool call.\nL154:   * Public source code with automated linting, tests, policy validation, and container builds.\nL155: ## What we learned\nL156: \nL157: We learned that reliable agentic systems need more than good model output. Identity propagation, model-region availability, secret boundaries, state transitions, and stale-result prevention all affect whether an agent can be trusted inside a real production workflow.\nL158: \nL159: We also learned that human-in-the-loop design is strongest when it is enforced by state and revision rules, not merely requested in a prompt.\nL160: ## What's next\nL161: \nL162: Next, we want to add transcript and subtitle ingestion, batch cue review, team assignments, source-quality controls, and deeper production-system integrations. We also plan to move the Agent Engine to a compatible multi-region endpoint so it can use the newest supported Gemini model while retaining the same evidence and approval guarantees.\nL163: \nL164: CUTLINE's long-term goal is simple: help documentary teams move faster without losing the chain of evidence behind the final cut.\nL165: ## Built With\nL166: \nL167:   * adk\nL168:   * agent\nL169:   * ai\nL170:   * api\nL171:   * artifact\nL172:   * build\nL173:   * cloud\nL174:   * engine\nL175:   * enterprise\nL176:   * firestore\nL177:   * 【24†gemini】\nL178:   * 【25†google】\nL179:   * iam\nL180:   * logging\nL181:   * manager\nL182:   * parallel\nL183:   * parallel-web\nL184:   * platform\nL185:   * 【26†python】\nL186:   * registry\nL187:   * run\nL188:   * search\nL189:   * vertex\nL190: \nL191: ## Try it out\nL192: \nL193:   * 【27†cutline-710287686519.us-central1.run.app†cutline-710287686519.us-central1.run.app】\nL194:   * 【28†github.com†github.com】\nL195:   * 【29†youtu.be†youtu.be】\nL196: #### Submitted to\nL197: \nL198:   * 【30†Agentic Cinema: The Blockbuster Hackathon†agentic-cinema.devpost.com】\nL199: \nL200: #### Created by\nL201: \nL202:   * 【31†Anupam Roy】\nL203: \nL204: 【19†Like†secure.devpost.com】\nL205: \nL206: ## Updates\nL207: \nL208: 【31†Anupam Roy】 started this project — 【32†Sep 09, 2026 04:12 PM EDT】\nL209: \nL210: Leave feedback in the comments!\nL211: \nL212: 【33†Log in†secure.devpost.com】 or 【34†sign up for Devpost†secure.devpost.com】 to join the conversation.\nL213: \nL214: ## Submission history\nL215: #### Devpost\nL216: \nL217:   * 【35†About†info.devpost.com】\nL218:   * 【36†Careers†info.devpost.com】\nL219:   * 【37†Contact†info.devpost.com】\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nMethod changes · FactUnion (https://factunion.com/methodology/changes/)\nciteturn82view2 [wordlim: 200] Crawled: today; Content type: text/html; Source: open({\"ref_id\":\"https://factunion.com/methodology/changes/\",\"lineno\":null}); Total lines: 114\nL0: 【0†Skip to the main content】\nL1: \nL2: 【1†FactUnion】 【2†How it works】【3†Pricing】\nL3: \nL4: 【4†Sign in】 【5†Check an episode】\nL5: \nL6: FactUnion Method 1.2\nL7: # Method changes\nL8: \nL9:   * 【6†Summary】\nL10:   * 【7†The full method】\nL11:   * 【8†Score】\nL12:   * 【9†Sources】\nL13:   * 【10†Same file】\nL14:   * 【11†Changes】\nL15:   * 【12†Fairness tests】\nL16: \nL17: A small change (wording of a prompt, additions to the source list, a bug fix) raises the second number. A change to the results, the score formula, the gates or the bar raises the first number. Old results always keep the version they were made with. Every version lists what changed, why, and how it did in our fairness tests.\nL18: ## FactUnion Method 1.4\nL19: Other languages (2026-10-27). Checks of fact sheets, scripts and recordings in Spanish, French, German, Portuguese and Hungarian, besides English. Nothing about English checks changed. The statuses, the score formula, the gates and the rules did not change.\nL20: For another language the reading model is told, on every step, the language of the material: claims, quoted words and passages stay exactly as written (never translated; a quoted passage is still checked word for word against the page), searches are written in the claim's language, and every sentence written for people (explanations, corrections, suggested rewrites) is written in the claim's language. Numbers are read the way each language writes them (8,7 millones; 1.234,5; 1 234,5).\nL21: Sentences are split correctly around German and Hungarian ordinals and dates (am 3. Oktober; 1848. március 15.) and Spanish and French punctuation. Recordings are transcribed in their own language. Labels on the report stay as they are.\nL22: Corrections are never guessed (2026-10-27). A test check corrected \"the first part of Don Quixote was published in 1705\" to 1622; the right year is 1605 (one source spoke of another edition). A subject often has several real dates: a first edition and later parts, the start and the end of a building, a law passed and in force.\nL23: Now a correction must rest on named passages that give the value of that same property, and when the sources give different values for it, or any source contradicts the corrected sentence when it is read again, no correction is written: the fact is removed with \"the value stated here is wrong, and the sources we read do not agree on the right one, so we did not guess\". A suggested rewrite of a script line follows the same rule (the line is still flagged; the suggestion is withheld).\nL24: ### Fairness test results for this version\nL25: \nL26:   * Mirror test (same claim, other side): not run yet.\nL27:   * Stability test (same result each time): not run yet. It must pass before this version checks any public episode.\nL28: ## FactUnion Method 1.3 · 18 October 2026\nL29: \nL30: What changed, and why. Creators asked to check a script before they record it, so a wrong claim can be fixed in the text instead of in the studio. Version 1.3 adds two kinds of check. The statuses, the score formula, the gates, the bar of 85 and the rules that decide a claim did not change: a script is read and researched exactly like a recording.\nL31:   * Script check. A script or transcript (plain text, Markdown, subtitles or a Word file; speaker names are optional) is split into numbered sentences and checked with the same method as a recording. Each claim that is not verified comes back with its sentence, where that sentence sits in the text, the result, the source, and a suggested rewrite of the line that keeps the writer's words and tone and says only what the sources state. When nothing true can replace a claim, the suggestion is to cut it.\nL32: The rewrite is a new instruction to our reading model, which is why the version number moved.\nL33:   * Final audio check. After recording, the final audio (or its transcript) is matched word by word to the checked script. When at least 90 percent of the words are the same, the script's results stand and only the lines that changed or are new are checked. When less than 90 percent is the same, the audio is checked in full as a new episode. The page says what was checked, for example: Script verified · audio matched 97% · 3 changed lines checked.\nL34:   * What is never decided by a model: which lines changed (a word by word comparison, case and punctuation ignored), the 90 percent line, and which findings ask for a correction (a claim sources contradict or a detail that is off) and which only ask for a look (a claim with weak support).\nL35:   * A changed line whose script version was verified and whose new version is not is always listed for a look, even when the new claim comes back as \"can't be checked\" rather than contradicted.\nL36:   * Test runs on two of our own episodes (test mode): the script checks found 80 and 82 claims (the checks of the same episodes' final audio found 66 and 83). Matching each episode's own final audio to its checked script came out 99.9 percent the same in seconds, with no research. Five lines edited by hand, one with a wrong number, were found as five changed lines and only they were checked; the wrong number was caught both times (once contradicted, once no longer verified).\nL37: ### Fairness test results for this version\nL38: \nL39:   * Mirror test (same claim, other side): not run yet.\nL40:   * Stability test (same result each time): not run yet. It must pass before this version checks any public episode.\nL41: ## FactUnion Method 1.2 · 17 October 2026\nL42: \nL43: What changed, and why. A fact sheet of true, sourced facts for one of our own episodes kept 4 of its 28 facts: most were removed because only one strong source, or only lower-weight sources, stated them, even though nothing said otherwise. That bar was stricter than any newsroom's. We changed it so a check is factual but not absurdly strict. The four statuses, the score formula, the gates and the bar of 85 did not change.\nL44:   * One reliable source that clearly states a claim is now enough for Verified, when no reliable source says otherwise. A reliable source is an official or original record, an established outlet, a reference or academic work, or an organisation's own page about itself. A second independent source still makes the result stronger, but it is no longer required.\nL45:   * Two or more independent lower-weight sites that our own searches found, and that agree with each other, also verify a claim when nothing we read says otherwise. Links from the piece's own description, a source the speaker named, an appeal, free hosting addresses and open platforms never count toward this. One lower-weight site alone still makes a claim Questionable.\nL46:   * A quote is on record when its exact words are on any page we read that states it. Words we find nowhere still make a quote Questionable (misattributed).\nL47:   * When our readings of the same pages still disagree, the reading by our stronger reading model decides, and the claim still shows that the readings did not agree. It is no longer marked Questionable for that reason alone. Serious claims about a named person or company keep their bar: two independent sources, including an official record.\nL48:   * A technical failure never counts against a claim. When a reading never comes back, we ask again; a reading that still did not come back does not vote. When a comparison step fails, we split it and ask again, and if part of it still fails, the readings stand without that comparison. If most of a step fails, the whole check runs again later.\nL49:   * Fact sheets for our own episodes: a fact stated only by a lower-weight source, with nothing against it, is kept with a short note naming the source. A fact the sources back only in part is trimmed to what they state and read again. A fact sources disagree about is corrected to what the reliable source says and read again.\nL50: A fact is removed only when a reliable source says otherwise and no correction holds, when nothing supports it after a real search, when a quote is not found, or when a key record would not open (shown as \"Couldn't be checked\", never as false).\nL51:   * Audit results for this version (calibration, test runs): on the fact sheet that kept 4 of 28 facts, 21 are kept now, 5 of them corrected to what the sources say (for example the right author of a 1955 paper); on the fact sheets of three published episodes, 133 of 149 facts are kept (89 percent), against 38 with the old bar on the same readings; of 12 true facts copied with one deliberate error each (a wrong year, number or name, an invented quote, a reversed claim, a false attribution), all 12 were caught: 5 removed and 7 corrected back to the true value.\nL52: ### Fairness test results for this version\nL53: \nL54:   * Mirror test (same claim, other side): not run yet.\nL55:   * Stability test (same result each time): not run yet. It must pass before this version checks any public episode.\nL56: ## FactUnion Method 1.1 · 14 October 2026\nL57: \nL58: What changed, and why. The first full test run on our own episode showed results we could check by hand and found wrong, so these rules changed. The statuses, the score formula, the gates and the thresholds did not.\nL59:   * Every judgment is now made by a language model that answers in a fixed form, never by hand-written text rules or word lists.\nL60: A small, fast model (Claude Haiku 5.5) compares each claim with each source passage: whether a number, date, time or amount in the source is the same as the one in the claim (it knows that 90 minutes is 1.5 hours and that \"September 5, 2025\" is a date), whether the page is the person's or organisation's own, whether the page got the claim from somewhere else, and whether a note about context is about the claim.\nL61: Our code then checks that every word the model says it copied from a page really is on that page, and does the arithmetic. This comparison runs in each of our repeated reads, so if the model is unsure, the reads disagree and we say so.\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nThe No-Spin Zone: How Journalistic Documentaries Check Their Facts | International Documentary Association (https://documentary.org/feature/no-spin-zone-how-journalistic-documentaries-check-their-facts)\nciteturn82view3 [wordlim: 200] Crawled: today; Content type: text/html; Source: open({\"ref_id\":\"https://documentary.org/feature/no-spin-zone-how-journalistic-documentaries-check-their-facts\",\"lineno\":28}); Total lines: 99\nL0: 【0†Skip to main content】\nL1: \nL2: [Input: Search]\nL3: \nL4: 【1†Scroll Down for content】\nL5: \nL6:   * 【2†All Articles】\nL7:   * 【3†All Issues】\nL8:   * 【4†Latest Issue】\nL9:   * 【5†Purchase†documentary.metalabel.com】\nL10: \nL11: 【6†Image: IDA Logo White】\nL12: \nL13: Featured Articles\nL14: \nL15: 【7†A Fresh 2025 Introduction to Documentary Budgeting and Scheduling (With New Templates!) By Robert Bahar】\nL16: \nL17: 【8†Greatest Docs of the 21st Century: Portrait of a Practice By Manuel Betancourt】\nL18: \nL19: 【9†Greatest Docs of the 21st Century: Toward New Forms of List-Making By Winnie Wang】\nL20: Mon, 09/18/2017\nL21: # The No-Spin Zone: How Journalistic Documentaries Check Their Facts\nL22: \nL23: By 【10†Nayantara Roy】\nL24: \nL25: * * *\nL26: A fact is a truth, that verifiable, reliable backbone of the documentarian's work. But in an era when information is filtered through the lens of belief and agenda, truth to one group may be heresy to another. Fact-checking has never been as complex or vital, whether to provide authenticity to a report or recapture a flailing public trust. Post 9/11, the documentarian has become a pivotal witness to a world in uproar, taking on many a journalistic mantle.\nL27: But according to the Center for Media and Social Impact's report Dangerous Documentaries: Reducing Risk When Telling Truth to Power, documentarians often find themselves with far fewer resources than the investigative journalist. An integrated model of fact-checking is one such resource, still in development within the documentarian's workflow.\nL28: News corporations that make documentaries enlist the crucial support of an in-house fact-checking team or external consultant. In independent documentary, fact-checking often falls to the documentarian in conjunction with a producer.\nL29: Filmmaker Lauren Rosenfeld Capps (Captive Radio, Rape in the Fields) notes, \"You need a paper trail and primary sources to back what you are saying.\" Capps creates a master document that contains all data that requires fact-checking, from dates and times to quotations and legal documents. \"Working in Google Spreadsheets, I add in each fact and create columns for corresponding primary sources,\" she explains. \"Each fact is linked to its related documents in a Dropbox folder, almost like a bibliography.\nL30: If it's a government document or a public record or digitized court document, you link to it online. If it's not online, you create a copy, put it in the Dropbox and refer to the page number and quote in the master document. If someone questions something, you go back to that line, see where you got that document from and how you can verify it. It's important to be meticulous, especially in more controversial investigative works.\nL31: If something is factually questionable and has no primary sources to back it up, even if it's a great piece for the film, it might not be worth using.\"\nL32: Capps would prefer not to fact-check alone. \"I like at least two eyes on a script, in addition to the producer or person writing it,\" she says. \"Most of the time I have been able to have at least one other person involved. We either split up each fact and tackle the script jointly or have a second set of eyes on the same set of lines or facts. If I'm writing a script that’s changing, it's good to have someone else updating the fact check documents and making note of what facts still need checking.\nL33: Within the [master] document, I have a column that tracks each fact to its checker's initials, whether an intern or producer. You refer back to that person if there are any questions later.\"\nL34: Investigative reporter, producer and independent fact-checker Jonathan Jones (Firestone & the Warlord) argues against documentarians fact-checking their own work. \"It's a [REDACTED_SECRET] of understanding about what fact-checking is; it's different from reporting,\" he maintains. \"In best-case scenarios, fact-checking should be done by someone somewhat independent of the piece, who is going to talk to the primary filmmaker or reporter and assess the credibility of sources.\nL35: That's hard to do as the primary reporter. I think the biggest fear in journalism is confirmation bias and not being able to see or catch errors because you're so close to the material.\"\nL36: Like Capps, Jones emphasizes the importance of a methodical workflow. \"If the filmmaker has organized notes and a somewhat full script, a fact-checker can relook at the material, for the larger fact-checking issues: Does it feel balanced and fair? Are we missing something? What are we particularly concerned about? Often that question will prompt you to do additional reporting. The basis of the film can be the hardest piece to nail down because you have to look at the totality of your gathered evidence.\"\nL37: Solo fact-checker for 【11†The New York Times Op-Docs†www.nytimes.com】, Lindsay Crouse has checked every Op-Docs piece since the debut of the online series. \"Fact-checking at Op-Docs occurs in two different areas,\" explains Crouse. \"One is the general nuts and bolts of any fact-checking that occurs at the Times. We consider ourselves to be the media institution of record, so everything we publish goes through that process, whether by our own reporters or by outside contributors.\nL38: We want to make sure [the pieces] can effectively become part of the public record and everything is verifiable and true. That means we run formal corrections on everything. We have viewers and readers who are our eyes and ears. They write in on anything they think may not be true, whether the spelling of someone's name or something that did not, in fact, happen. My job is to first pre-empt those kinds of corrections.\nL39: I vet names, ages, any objective facts, by going to the source and making sure that everything is factually true.\nL40: \"The second stage,\" Crouse continues, \"is a deeper fact-checking for transparency, and it's new ground for the Times for video, especially by external contributors. Sometimes I get on the phone with the filmmaker and ask them the who, what, when, where. Make sure no one was paid. What dates it was filmed. Is anything in the film fictional or staged? Most films come to us when they have already been shot, so I am mostly retracing the filmmaker's work.\nL41: I make sure that everything presented in filmic form, in the filmmaker's depicted recording, actually occurred in that chronology, that nothing has been mashed up for the sake of telling a better story. For photos, it's making sure that the image is correct, that it is a picture of the intended person and not an example photo. I ensure transparency, in terms of not necessarily plagiarism but the veracity or originality of story.\nL42: If, for example, a story has already been reported by the Times, I check that the documentarian's reporting adds to the story, rather than just repackaging something original by another reporter. Then, with the filmmaker, I double-check with experts and other sources.\"\nL43: 【12†The New Yorker†newyorker.com】 has a team of 17 fact-checkers, headed by Peter Canby, not including three Web-checkers who fact-check videos. Says Canby, \"You deconstruct a piece mentally and try to break it down into its sources. You try to figure out where everything came from, who's quoted, to whom things are attributed, what reports are mentioned, what incidents are described, who was spoken with and who wasn't spoken with.\nL44: If there are long swatches of narrative with no source, you look at those with special attention because you have to figure out where all that came from. Then you ask the writer who to talk to, who not to talk to or be careful with, what sources you can use. The writer sends us notes and transcripts, which we read before making calls to sensitive subjects—say, somebody who the writer has coaxed information out of and who may get feisty and start denying things that you know he said.\"\nL45: News corporations are armed with tools for best practices. LexisNexis is used extensively. \"We've been very successful at finding people that other people are having trouble finding,\" says Canby. \"Nexis is almost startlingly invasive. You can find everything, including whole social security numbers and things that shouldn't be there but are.\nL46: We have access to Lexis and PACER, which give us entire legal histories, and a commercial service called Leadership Directories, which gives us an amazing directory of Washington phone numbers. But it doesn't help you if you're trying to reach the White House Communications Office and nobody is willing to answer any questions. We have People Locator, which is expensive. Celebrity Service gives us names of agents for even minor celebrities.\nL47: A number of years ago, I tried to work out a list of the most consulted sites. Within six months, most of those sites had been superseded by something else. Ultimately, people pass along sources by word of mouth.\"\nL48: For documentarians who would like to work with 【13†Frontline†www.pbs.org】, creating hard-hitting investigative works for the news network, Managing Editor Andrew Metz recommends \"setting out with the ambition to understand and cite multiple perspectives and sources of your subject, to spend time in the investigative process.\" At Frontline, the documentarian is considered a journalist.\nL49: Metz describes the process as one that works with \"people who are reporters, who approach things with a tough, skeptical, open mind, determined to do extensive research and reporting, and not attached to any particular point of view, organization or advocacy. We go towards the complexity of subjects that appear confusing and are hard to decipher. That's what you're after when you produce on Frontline.\nL50: You accumulate so much source material and such a deep understanding of your subject that it allows you to build a fact-checking process for yourself and for our external review. We require sound, narration and picture to be vetted before it comes to us. That doesn't mean that when we look at it we’re not going to raise more questions. The goal is to be fair and accurate. We just want to avoid the rare instances where we end up having to do our own reporting, from scratch, with entirely new sources.\"\nL51: Metz emphasizes the publicly available guidelines that journalists and filmmakers who work with Frontline are expected to follow. \"These long-standing, guiding principles continue to push and guide us. We do long-form documentary film, but at heart we are journalists. Documentary film is a wide, big family and some people don't identify as journalists. They use the tools of journalism but don't come after the endeavor as journalists.\nL52: Frontline is about fairness, rigor, reporting against your reporting, examining contrary information thoroughly.\"\nL53: Capps notes that filmmakers without institutional support have far fewer fact-checking resources. \"Conversations about resources for freelancers need to continue, whether it's legal review resources or who to go to when you don't have organizational backing.\" For filmmakers like Capps, who often fact-check independently, using the right tools is key. \"For stories about the criminal justice system or legal cases, I have gone to courthouses to get physical records,\" recounts Capps.\n"}]

You can find everything, including whole social security numbers and things that shouldn't be there but are.\nL46: We have access to Lexis and PACER, which give us entire legal histories, and a commercial service called Leadership Directories, which gives us an amazing directory of Washington phone numbers. But it doesn't help you if you're trying to reach the White House Communications Office and nobody is willing to answer any questions. We have People Locator, which is expensive. We go towards the complexity of subjects that appear confusing and are hard to decipher. That's what you're after when you produce on Frontline.\nL50: You accumulate so much source material and such a deep understanding of your subject that it allows you to build a fact-checking process for yourself and for our external review. We require sound, narration and picture to be vetted before it comes to us. That doesn't mean that when we look at it we’re not going to raise more questions. The goal is to be fair and accurate. We just want to avoid the rare instances where we end up having to do our own reporting, from scratch, with entirely new sources.\"\nL51: Metz emphasizes the publicly available guidelines that journalists and filmmakers who work with Frontline are expected to follow. \"These long-standing, guiding principles continue to push and guide us. We do long-form documentary film, but at heart we are journalists. Documentary film is a wide, big family and some people don't identify as journalists. They use the tools of journalism but don't come after the endeavor as journalists.\nL52: Frontline is about fairness, rigor, reporting against your reporting, examining contrary information thoroughly.\"\nL53: Capps notes that filmmakers without institutional support have far fewer fact-checking resources. \"Conversations about resources for freelancers need to continue, whether it's legal review resources or who to go to when you don't have organizational backing.\" For filmmakers like Capps, who often fact-check independently, using the right tools is key. \"For stories about the criminal justice system or legal cases, I have gone to courthouses to get physical records,\" recounts Capps.\nL54: \"Deposition videos taken under oath are powerful visual tools in investigative pieces. Even if you don't use them, transcripts of depositions and court testimony can be helpful in backing up facts.\"\nL55: Fact-checking is also a vital protection against liability and may ensure protection for the documentarian and her sources when the subject matter is sensitive. \"I did a film about minors facing abuse in the criminal justice system,\" Capps recalls. \"It can be difficult to have those conversations. But a lot of the stories that we told and the claims made were backed by legal documents and public records.\" Fact-checking can begin sometimes as early as the conception of the idea.\nL56: \"Even before you begin your film or before an interview, it's useful to think about what documents or research will help to fact-check what the interviewees are saying or what the narration is going to be,\" says Capps.\nL57: \"The fact-checker should be on board by the time the filmmaker is getting a cut together,\" advises Jones. \"I discuss the subject with the filmmaker, maybe watch a cut and set up a schedule of delivery dates. You don't want to be bugging the filmmaker every time you hit a road block on your facts or something needs to be changed. I get a feel for the script and its fairness and balance.\nL58: Then I do what I call 'sweeps.' I underline all factual assertions made in the script, and begin to fact-check by accessing the source material. With Frontline, it's between one to three months [of fact-checking] before a piece locked. You are basically working backwards from when you want to be locked.\"\nL59: Op-Docs videos initially come to Crouse in rough-cut stage. \"The best someone can possibly do when they come to me is have a script that's fully annotated in terms of sourcing for each part of an interview,\" she notes. \"'This fact came from this Times article,' for example.\" Crouse recommends a thorough consulting of experts in the documentarian's subject field. \"I reach out to Harvard or Princeton or Yale to find a scholar who is familiar with the topic.\nL60: The important thing in researching and vetting is to try to figure out the other side of the story ahead of time. See who is going to disagree with your information. Go find that person. You don't want to publish it, have them discover it later and then realize that you have no idea where something came from.\nL61: The best-case scenario for me is to not discover anything in my fact-checking that the filmmaker did not already know.\" Crouse cautions the documentarian submitting to Op-Docs in allowing any singular stance to infiltrate the material. \"Sometimes we discover that perhaps a filmmaker was a little too credulous or a little too advocacy-based without thinking enough about the other side of the story for us to want to run it and endorse their perspective,\" she says.\nL62: \"Of course, Op-Docs films have a strong point of view. Filmmakers don't have to create a balanced perspective, but they do have to show that they are aware and engaging with all the other sides to the story.\"\nL63: Factual errors are sometimes the product of repetition and belief without verification. \"Many news reports and academic studies say that the Firestone plantation in Liberia is a million acres,\" Jones observes. \"It's completely wrong but keeps getting repeated in publications. So even though you can find sources backing up your assertion that it is a million acres, it's an error. A fact-checker would hopefully be able to prevent errors like that.\nL64: The more circumspect you are of sources, the more sources you need. A primary source is a much better source than a bunch of secondary sources, where you need additional steps to verify that the information is true. Depending on your time and resources, your fact-checker can do additional interviewing and site visits, becoming a kind of reporter to fill in the gaps. It also takes a lot of coordination with the filmmaker so that you don't mess up the process for them.\"\nL65: Canby, too, stresses the importance of primary sources. \"The key to good journalism is original sources, as much as possible. The more you have secondary and tertiary sources, the less original and interesting and thorough a piece is. We find that really well reported pieces are ones where reporters persist for the right answer from somebody who’s actually involved in their subject.\"\nL66: For the documentarian to be prepared for self or institutional review, best practices include organization, appropriate naming conventions, security and storage. At Frontline, highly sensitive material is rote and documentarians are required to provide all sourcing. Metz works in close conjunction with a lawyer. \"He reviews all films long before broadcast so that any issues that he might cite or question may be addressed,\" says Metz. \"Filmmakers must be prepared for external and legal review.\nL67: We use encryption and other ways of getting sensitive material [from the filmmaker]. Not every film requires that. In all cases, we have access to all interviews and transcripts. Any underline reporting, documentation, video, depositions and visual elements are open for review.\"\nL68: The relationship between fact-checker and documentarian may become tenuous when ownership and boundaries come into question. \"As a filmmaker doing point-of-view journalism, sometimes a fact-checker can find things that don't fit that point of view,\" observes Jones. \"If I'm saying that the sources you are using to make an assertion are not correct or strong enough, that inevitably creates some tension. An important part is also presenting ways to fix it.\nL69: Ultimately, it's up to the filmmaker whether they want to accept, not accept or modify your changes.\"\nL70: \"We don't want to undo the reporter's work,\" notes Canby. \"We're very mindful of not wandering into things and creating a mess that wasn't there—for example, have [a subject] deny something that the person actually said. There's a lot of conflict built into [checking] because you're taking somebody’s material and combing through their sources. They may see things one way and you may gradually come to see that maybe it's not quite the way you see it in there.\nL71: When we see something important, we focus on it and make sure that people are willing to hear us out.\"\nL72: At a news network, final editorial say usually lies with the organization. There is often a liability waiver and set rules that the documentarian must subscribe to. \"The best filmmakers and journalists recognize that a rigorous process where things are examined, makes their work and Frontline stronger,\" says Metz. \"It makes it harder, but the most experienced people recognize that we're all pushing in the same direction.\"\nL73: Says Crouse, of Op-Docs, \"Sometimes we have had disagreements [with filmmakers] about sources and the extent to which a source is sufficient, and we have killed a few pieces. If I don't feel that I can answer questions about the veracity and relevance of a piece, then we can't publish it. We have had filmmakers who aren't able to prove that their piece is accurate to the point where I am comfortable publishing it with potential implications that might occur.\"\nL74: Capps advocates communication as key to working with news corporations. \"Have conversations before you even get started when you're producing or creating something for an organization,\" says the filmmaker. \"Be very clear what their expectations, formats and deliverables are going to be. If it is investigative, and you have never done anything like that before, consult with an investigative journalist before starting the project.\nL75: Don't be afraid to talk to other people and see what they have done in the past.\"\nL76: The work of fact-checking is more complex than perusing information that may be boiled down to data. Opinion and fact may overlap. Argument and images may miss vital context.\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nTrint MCP Connector (https://info.trint.com/knowledge/trint-mcp-connector)\nciteturn83view1 [wordlim: 200] Crawled: today; Content type: text/html; Source: open({\"ref_id\":\"https://info.trint.com/knowledge/trint-mcp-connector\",\"lineno\":null}); Total lines: 78\nL0: 【0†iframe†www.googletagmanager.com】\nL1: \nL2: 【1†Skip to content】\nL3: \nL4: English Show submenu for translations\nL5: \nL6: 【2†Customer portal】\nL7: \nL8: Open main navigation Close main navigation\nL9: \nL10:   * English Show submenu for translations\nL11: \nL12:   * 【3†Customer portal】\nL13:   * 【4†Go to trint.com†trint.com】\nL14: \nL15: 【4†Go to trint.com†trint.com】\nL16: \nL17: Looking to do more with Trint? [Input: Search for answers] [Input] [Input]\nL18: \nL19:   * There are no suggestions because the search field is empty.\nL20: \nL21: # Trint MCP Connector\nL22: ## Learn all about the Trint MCP Connector, how to use it and how it functions within your workflow.\nL23: \nL24: An MCP server is an open standard that lets AI agents and assistants access external tools and data sources. The Trint MCP Connector uses this open standard, so that journalists, researchers, and media teams already working with Claude or another AI assistant, can bring Trint’s capabilities directly into their workflows, without switching context.\nL25: This is an implementation of Anthropic’s Model Context Protocol (MCP) that makes Trint’s capabilities available to any MCP-compatible AI tool. Trint is not just a standalone app, but a tool inside your AI workflow.\nL26: \nL27: This support guide will cover:\nL28: \nL29:   * 【5†What does the Trint MCP Connector do】\nL30:   * 【6†Why we've created it】\nL31:   * 【7†How to set up the Trint MCP Connector】\nL32:   * 【8†FAQs】\nL33: ### What does the Trint MCP Connector do?\nL34: \nL35: The Trint MCP Connector exposes a set of tools that a connected AI agent can call on the user’s behalf. These tools allow the AI to:\nL36: \nL37:   * Get transcript content – Retrieve the full text of any Trint file the user has access to\nL38: \nL39:   * Search – Query across a Trint workspace or specific folder to find relevant files or moments\nL40: \nL41:   * Find quotes – Identify the best quotes according to a prompt the user provides.\nL42:   * View and play quotes – Render a quote within an agent and playback. Open a quote on 【9†app.trint.com†app.trint.com】 to edit and comment.\nL43:   * Export – Download a completed transcript or selected moments as edl, srt and docx\nL44: \nL45: Together, these tools enable an end-to-end workflow: transcribe - find - extract - draft - export - entirely within an AI assistant or agentic pipeline.\nL46: \nL47: Keep your eyes peeled for even more updates on the Trint MCP Connector, as we roll out more features and improvements.\nL48: ### Why have we created a Trint MCP Connector?\nL49: \nL50: General purpose AI tools can do some of the functions of Trint, such as transcribe, summarise, find key quotes. But there are some things they can’t do. And because Trint is an AI tool workflow built for media production and newsrooms, we’re able to take what works in Trint and amplify it with the MCP Connector.\nL51: \nL52: Here's an example of when the Trint MCP Connector is most beneficial:\nL53: You’ve got a quote from a speaker that you’re checking and verifying. With the Trint MCP Connector you can get the audio of the quote along with the response from the AI agent so that you can double check the transcript was correct while hearing the soundbite out loud, making sure it’s right for your content.\nL54: \nL55: 【10†Image】\nL56: \nL57: And every quote always has a mapping link back to the original transcript so you can easily open it up and work with it in the Trint UI.\nL58: ### How to set up the Trint MCP Connector\nL59: \nL60: Setting the Trint MCP Connector with your AI tools is simple and can be done in under approximately 10 minutes.\nL61: \nL62: Check out our step-by-step guide to setting up the Trint MCP Connector 【11†here.】\nL63: ### FAQs\nL64: \nL65:   * Does the MCP only work with Claude?\nL66: No, it works with any MCP-compatible AI framework that supports the standard.\nL67:   * Can I make my own skills to use within the Trint MCP Connector?\nL68: Yes, you can create a set of different skills within the MCP, depending on your workflow processes and what you want your output to be.\nL69:   * How does this affect my data security?\nL70: The MCP operates on behalf of the authenticated Trint user – it only accesses files that the user already has permission to see, governed by the same role-based access as the Trint platform.\nL71: We hope you have found this article useful. If you have any questions, please reach out to us at support@trint.com and our team will be happy to help.\nL72: \nL73: trint.com Support Hub\nL74: \nL75: Copyright © 2026, Trint\nL76: \nL77: 【0†iframe†www.googletagmanager.com】\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nComparison Viewer | Frame.io V4 Knowledge Center (https://help.frame.io/en/articles/9952618-comparison-viewer)\nciteturn83view2 [wordlim: 200] Crawled: today; Content type: text/html; Source: open({\"ref_id\":\"https://help.frame.io/en/articles/9952618-comparison-viewer\",\"lineno\":null}); Total lines: 69\nL0: Copyright (c) 2023, Intercom, Inc. This Font Software is licensed under the SIL Open Font License, Version 1.1.【0†Skip to main content】\nL1: \nL2: [Select]\nL3: \nL4: English\nL5: \nL6: Search`⌘K`\nL7: \nL8:   * 【1†Frame.io Explained】\nL9: \nL10:   * 【2†Upload and Organize】\nL11: \nL12:   * 【3†Collaboration and Playback】\nL13: \nL14:   * 【4†Account Settings】\nL15:   * 【5†Frame.io C2C】\nL16: \nL17:   * 【6†Frame.io Drive & Mounted Storage】\nL18: \nL19:   * 【7†Integrations and API】\nL20: \nL21:   * 【8†Enterprise】\nL22: \nL23:   * 【9†Security, Privacy, and Terms】\nL24: \nL25:   * 【10†Frame.io Legacy (V3) Article Collection】\nL26: # Comparison Viewer\nL27: \nL28: Written by Jared\nL29: \nL30: September 9, 2026\nL31: \nL32: What's new in V4:\nL33: \nL34:   * Compare any two assets regardless of file type\nL35: \nL36:   * Unlink, Overlay, and Pixel Difference views are now available when comparing static assets\nL37: The Comparison Viewer in Frame.io V4 is the best way to compare all your assets side-by-side and make it easier to see the differences between edits. This view comes with the ability to leave comments on either asset and compare details with linked zoom and playback controls. This view supports all video, image, audio, PDF, and MS Office types.\nL38: Select two assets in your Project, click the three-dot menu and then select Compare Assets to launch into the Comparison view. If you want to compare two version stacked assets, select the option to Compare Versions in the Project page or in the player page of the version stacked assets. You can also select a single asset and a version stacked asset and choose which of these two options to compare. You can also do this by selecting the Compare icon at the bottom bar of your Project page.\nL39: Note: You can compare any two assets in Frame.io. They do not have to be the same file type to use the Comparison Viewer.\nL40: \nL41: Comments can be made while in the Comparison view. Click on the left or right asset header you wish to leave feedback on to ensure the feedback is being left on the correct asset.\nL42: ​\nL43: ## Static asset comparison\nL44: \nL45: With static assets (non-video), there are a few additional features that can be done alongside the comparison viewer. All tools are available at the top of any static asset in the comparison viewer.\nL46: ### Unlink controls\nL47: \nL48: There is an option to Unlink controls on the top of the viewer as a link icon. When linked, you will be able to control both assets with any zoom and repositioning actions made. If unlinked, the two assets will act independently, allowing you to select the asset you want to move around while the other will stay untouched. Zoom on trackpads by pinching-to-zoom, or Cmd+scrolling with your mouse on Mac.\nL49: Clicking the \"x\" icon on either asset will resume single-asset viewing in your player page.\nL50: ### Overlay view\nL51: \nL52: When comparing two similar images, click Show/Hide Slider to combine the two assets. Use the slider bar (found in the center of the image) and drag-to-reveal both images overlayed on top of each other to see a more precise visual comparison.\nL53: \nL54: Note: Static assets must have the same dimensions to use this feature.\nL55: ### Pixel difference view\nL56: \nL57: When selecting the Show/Hide Differences option in the comparison viewer, the assets on the right side will show in black and white. When you click on this asset, it will highlight the pixel differences between the two assets and letting you know exactly what has changed between them.\nL58: \nL59: Note: Long pressing the layer on the right will hide the visual differences and show the original version.\nL60: With the overlay view active, you can use the slider to drag between the two combined assets to compare differences.\nL61: \nL62: Did this answer your question?\nL63: \nL64: Disappointed Reaction Neutral Reaction Smiley Reaction\nL65: \nL66: On this page\nL67: \nL68:   * 【11†Static asset comparison】\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nAI-Powered Media Asset Management | Axle AI (https://www.axle.ai/)\nciteturn83view3 [wordlim: 200] Crawled: today; Content type: text/html; Source: open({\"ref_id\":\"https://www.axle.ai/\",\"lineno\":null}); Total lines: 302\nL0: 【0†Products】【1†Industries】【2†Contact Us】【3†Blog】\nL1: \nL2: 【4†Axle AI Cloud login†cloud.axle.ai】【5†Axledit login†app.axledit.com】\nL3: \nL4: 【6†Book a demo†calendly.com】\nL5: \nL6: 【7†Visit us at NABShow NY, booth 158, for the US debut of Axle AI FOCUS】\nL7: \nL8: AI-Powered media asset management\nL9: – 100% on premise!\nL10: \nL11: Semantic search with custom metadata and workflows,\nL12: built on local AI engines that you choose and control.\nL13: Trainable faces, scene understanding, transcription,\nL14: logo and text recognition and more.\nL15: 【6†Schedule a 30 minute demo†calendly.com】【8†See how it works】\nL16: \nL17: [Input: Email Address][Input]\nL18: \nL19: Thank you! It’s a good idea to do your fact checking at fine cut stage, as you are finalizing your narration.\nShould certain scenes be dumped?\n\nUpdate Doc Plot Map^{TM}.\n\nFine Cut\n\nIn composing the fine cut, it’s recommended that you rescreen your rushes if you have time, or at least reread your transcripts.  Footage and sound bites that escaped your attention the first time around may jump out at your now that your know your structure and sequences.\n\nThe fine cut will be viewed by advisors, funders, test audiences.  Give them an accurate, updated transcript.\n\nThe length should be within three percent of the final TRT.\n\nThe film’s structure should now be in place.\n\nInclude film’s title treatment, temp music, temp narration, placeholders for every single forthcoming shot, window dubs of archival material.\n\nInclude effects and micro cutting.\n\nNo credits, black holes or jump cuts (unless planned for final film).\n\nIt’s a good idea to do your fact checking at fine cut stage, as you are finalizing your narration.\n\nUpdate Doc Plot Map^{TM} if needed.\n\nLocked Picture\n\nLocked picture means just that:  from now on there will be no more changes to the video part of your film or to the length of your timeline.\n\nAfter you lock picture, you will overlay the following video:\n\n–       Master archival material\n\n–       Final graphics and animation;\n\nYou will lay back the following audio:\n\n–       Final composed music\n\n–       Final narration recording\n\n–       Final sound effects (FX).\n\n \n\nCongratulations! Now you can move on to the “online edit”, which means color correction and a sound mix.--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nSaving files and version history (https://info.trint.com/knowledge/saving-files-and-version-history-trint-help-center)\nciteturn84search1 [wordlim: 200] Crawled: last month; # Saving files and version history\n\n## Automatic saving and how to restore previous versions of your Trint\n\n# Saving files and version history\n\n## Automatic saving and how to restore previous versions of your Trint\n\nUpdated over a week ago\n\nTrint automatically saves your files at regular intervals such as when you make an edit. This guide will show you how to know when your file is saved and how to access older versions.\n\nThis support guide will cover:\n\n  * Automatic saving\n\n  * Version history\n\n### Automatic saving\n\nWhen you make a change to a transcript, caption or Story file, Trint will automatically save your progress and allow you to access older versions if you want to go back in time.\n\nYou can identify the last time your file was saved by looking at the “Version History” button on the right-hand side.\n\n### Version history\n\nYou can see and access any previous version of your Trint file by clicking on the “Version History” button and selecting a version to restore it. \n\nPlease note, version history is not available for Story or caption files.\n\nImage: Screenshot 2025-03-13 at 3.36.47 PM\n\nTrint saves automatically at regular intervals. When you make an edit to the file, the time the file last saved briefly pops up under Version History on the right.\n\nThis will create a copy of your file from the dated save and not erase your current version. You can choose to rename and share this older version with collaborators who have access to the main Trint file.--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nEmotional Intensity (https://newdocediting.com/wp-content/uploads/2018/03/Stages-of-Post-production.pdf)\nciteturn84search7 [wordlim: 200] Published: 11 months ago; It's a good idea to do your fact checking at fine cut stage, as you are finalizing\nEmotional Intensity \n \nThe film's structure should now be in place. \nInclude film's title treatment, temp music, temp narration, placeholders for every \nsingle forthcoming shot, window dubs of archival material. \nInclude effects and micro cutting. \nNo credits, black holes or jump cuts (unless planned for final film). \nIt's a good idea to do your fact checking at fine cut stage, as you are finalizing \nyour narration. \nUpdate Doc Plot MapTM if needed. \nLocked Picture \nLocked picture means just that:  from now on there will be no more changes to \nthe video part of your film or to the length of your timeline. \nAfter you lock picture, you will overlay the following video: -    master archival \nmaterial -    final graphics and animation; - You will lay back the following \naudio: -    final composed music -    final narration recording -    final sound FX \n \nNew Doc Editing, LLC  Karen Everett 1247 Potrero Avenue  |  San Francisco, CA  |  94110  |  T: 415.601.4613  |  karen@newdocediting.com  |  \nwww.newdocediting.com--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\n (https://newdocediting.com/successful-rough-cut-screening-part-1/)\nciteturn84search2 [wordlim: 200] Crawled: last week; If you need to do some key fact checking, or if your essay-style doc depends on an argument that one of your advisors deems invalid, you’ll want to handle these problems now–before heading into the fine cut.\n### Successful Rough Cut Screening, Part 1\n\nThis two-part guide will explain how to conduct a successful rough cut screening with your nerves intact.\n\nWho to Invite\n\nThere are three types of people you should show your rough cut to:  1) experts on the topic who serve as your advisors; 2) seasoned documentary professionals, and 3) people representative of your film’s target audience.\n\nEach audience should be handled differently.  This guide is geared more toward showing your film to a group of everyday people who will likely want to see your film when it’s released.  But let me first say a word about the first two groups. Experts on your subject matter, including any advisors, should watch your documentary mid-post production with an eye out for accuracy and balance.  If you need to do some key fact checking, or if your essay-style doc depends on an argument that one of your advisors deems invalid, you’ll want to handle these problems now–before heading into the fine cut.\n\nAs for documentary professionals, including filmmakers, editors and story editors (consultants), this group of peers should watch your rough cut electronically (alone) or at their own special screening, so they don’t have to dumb down terms like “protagonist”, “story arc”, etc.  --------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nDocumentary Editing (https://www.newdocediting.com/ebook/DocEditBook.pdf)\nciteturn84search8 [wordlim: 200] Published: 15.7 years ago; It’s a good idea to do your fact checking at fine cut stage, as you are finalizing your narration.\nThe fine cut maximizes your editor’s micro-cutting and aesthetic skills. It should contain no credits (still), black holes or jump cuts (unless planned for final film).\n\nIt’s a good idea to do your fact checking at fine cut stage, as you are finalizing your narration. Update Doc Plot Map if needed.\n\n## Locked Picture\n\nLocked picture means just that: from now on there will be no more changes to the video part of your film or to the length of your timeline.\n\nAfter you lock picture, you will overlay the following video:\n- master archival material\n- final graphics;\n- animation\n\nYou will lay back the following audio:\n- final composed music\n- final narration recording\n- final sound FX\n\nOnce the fine cut is complete, you are ready for what used to be called “onlining”, or these days, “finishing.” That means adding the final audio mix and color correction. Films with adequate budgets will frequently phase out their editorial staff and move their project to a high-end editing facility where technicians finesse the EQ, brightness, contrast and color saturation, as well as output and transfer to various tape and digital formats.\n\n13\n\n© 2010, Karen Everett, All Rights Reserved, NewDocEditing.com--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nEmotional Intensity (https://newdocediting.com/editing_course1/DocumentaryRoughCutScreening.pdf)\nciteturn84search9 [wordlim: 200] Published: last year; If you need to do some key fact checking, or if your essay- ... handle these problems now--before heading into the fine cut.\nEmotional Intensity \n \n \n \n \n \nEach audience should be handled differently.  This guide is geared more toward showing \nyour film to a group of everyday people who will likely want to see your film when it’s \nreleased.  But let me first say a word about the first two groups. Experts on your subject \nmatter, including any advisors, should watch your documentary mid-post production with an \neye out for accuracy and balance.  If you need to do some key fact checking, or if your essay-\nstyle doc depends on an argument that one of your advisors deems invalid, you’ll want to \nhandle these problems now--before heading into the fine cut.  \n \nAs for documentary professionals, including filmmakers, editors and story editors \n(consultants), this group of peers should watch your rough cut at their own special screening, \nso they can talk shop without alienating anyone or having to dumb down their use of terms \nlike “protagonist”, “story arc”, etc.  For your third group, roughly a dozen people who are \nrepresentative of your documentary’s intended audience, your approach will require special \ncare outlined in this article. \n \n \nWhere to Hold the Screening \n \nWhile it’s OK to give advisors and documentary professionals a \ncopy or link to your rough cut and ask them to get back to you, \nfilmmakers with a budget for screenings may want to rent out a \nscreening room at a local filmmaking agency. \n \n \nThis arrangement builds esteem for your film, encourages invitees to take the event seriously, \nand creates a nice pre-release buzz for your film. You can now see the file size on version history, so you can make quick decisions on what versions you choose to restore.\n\n \n\nTranscript display on missing media (mobile)\n\nIf an audio or video is missing, we still provide any available transcript.\n\nBetter handling of upload cancellations (mobile)\n\nRecordings or imported files are not deleted from the app when logging out.\n\nAdded context in account page\n\nWe now provide more context on the trial time and number of seats available, creating more transparency and guidance for users.\n\n### Release Update - Nov 11, 2022\n\n### Improved 👩‍🔬\n\nBetter handling of imported files\n\nThe mobile app no longer crashes when uploading a file you recorded on a previous version.\n\nFile size on version history\n\nYou can now see the file size on version history, so you can make quick decisions on what versions you choose to restore.\n\n### Bugs 🐞\n\n  * Fixed an issue that affected exporting via the Trint player\n\n \n\n### Release Update - Nov 4, 2022\n\n### Improved 👩‍🔬\n\nView files shared with you on mobile\n\nYou can now easily see all files shared with you via the mobile app (both iOS and Android) via the “Show all files” screen under “Share with me.”\n\nCommunicate with our support team with ease\n\nYou can now copy/paste your TrintID from the mobile app, so you can easily communicate with our support team quickly and provide them with the information they need to help you.\n\n### Bugs 🐞\n\n  * Fixed an issue that affected exporting via the Trint player--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nAdobe Workfront Integration Overview | Frame.io V4 Knowledge Center (https://help.frame.io/en/articles/12242607-adobe-workfront-integration-overview)\nciteturn84search6 [wordlim: 200] Crawled: today;   * Streamlined approvals — Request stakeholder approvals directly from Workfront, with Frame.io as the review surface — including unlimited video reviews. ...   * Live status sync — See which assets are approved and which need revisions, with approval status updated across both platforms. ... For example, any time you edit or move a asset/folder/version stack on Frame.io, this change will be reflected automatically in Workfront. ... This guide will explore the new Story interface and show you how it helps you create powerful content.\nL25: \nL26: This support guide will cover:\nL27: \nL28:   * 【5†Creating a Story】\nL29: \nL30:   * 【6†Editorial tools】\nL31: \nL32:   * 【7†Adding quotes】\nL33: \nL34:   * 【8†Editing quotes】\nL35: \nL36:   * 【9†Story FAQs】\nL37: \nL38: * * *\nL39: ### Creating a Story\nL40: \nL41: First go to the Trint dashboard and click the New Story button in the navigation bar.  You will now see our new Story interface, which has been designed to help you easily work between transcripts and your Story without needing multiple tabs on your browser. Download or update at 【20†claude.com/download†claude.com】.\nL50: \nL51:   * Web and Claude Mobile: Pro, Max, Ultra, and Team plans (in beta on Team), and Enterprise plans when an owner enables it.\nL52:   * Claude in Chrome side panel: All paid plans. On Enterprise plans, an owner needs to enable Claude in Chrome and Cowork in the cloud. See 【21†Get started with Claude in Chrome】 for more information.\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nMartin Edström | QNAP (US) (https://www.qnap.com/en-us/customer-stories/tvs-h1288x-martin-edstrom)\nciteturn84view3 [wordlim: 200] Crawled: today; Content type: text/html; Source: open({\"ref_id\":\"https://www.qnap.com/en-us/customer-stories/tvs-h1288x-martin-edstrom\",\"lineno\":918}); Total lines: 1033\nL896: ### Start a container on NAS\nL897: \nL898: Run your microservices using Container Station that integrates Docker®, LXD, and Kata Containers lightweight virtualization technologies.\nL899: \nL900: 【326†Learn More】\nL901: \nL902:   * 【327†Image】\nL903: \nL904: ### Backup, Restoration & Sync for NAS\nL905: \nL906: Use Hybrid Backup Sync to double up your protection by quickly backing up your NAS to remote and cloud storage spaces – ideal for building a complete 3-2-1 backup and recovery plan.\nL907: \nL908: 【194†Learn More】\nL909: \nL910:   * 【328†Image】\nL911: ### NAS storage management\nL912: \nL913: Use Storage & Snapshots to create NAS storage space, manage RAID configuration, snapshots, and SSD cache settings, and monitor drive health with minimal efforts.\nL914: \nL915: 【329†Learn More】\nL916: ## Challenges\nL917: \nL918: Martin’s storytelling has evolved from 360° video to interactive, 3D-scanned experiences that let the user fully walk around his stories. This technical leap has enabled him to not only create films, but now game-like experiences. Together with National Geographic, this interactivity has for example been used in 3D scans of Stonehenge.\nL919: The technology is not only for sharing epic locations. In a science project in Kyrgyzstan, Martin and the team managed to accelerate new scientific progress through their technology. By creating 3D models of scans of cave systems and entire mountains, the scientists could identify new caves and further explore the geology of the country. At the same time, the story of their work and progress was brought to life.\nL920: \nL921: 【330†Image】 【331†Image】\nL922: Drones and helicopters are often utilized to capture tens of thousands of images in every direction, with the 360° videos created from footage of eight to twelve cameras working in sync. The steps to realize the 3D models are photogrammetry or the more avant-garde methods of Gaussian Splatting, resulting in good-looking and realistic models. Input and output are both vast amounts of data; irreplaceable data that must be accessible during the editing process.\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nGitHub - Anupam0202/cutline: Version-bound documentary fact review with Gemini and Parallel Search · GitHub (https://github.com/Anupam0202/cutline)\nciteturn84view4 [wordlim: 200] Crawled: today; Content type: text/html; Source: find({\"ref_id\":\"turn83view4\",\"pattern\":\"invalidat\"}); Total lines: 414\nNo matching text found for \"invalidat\"--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nGitHub - Anupam0202/cutline: Version-bound documentary fact review with Gemini and Parallel Search · GitHub (https://github.com/Anupam0202/cutline)\nciteturn84view5 [wordlim: 200] Crawled: today; Content type: text/html; Source: find({\"ref_id\":\"turn83view4\",\"pattern\":\"Tests\"}); Total lines: 414\nL172: Name  | Name  | Last commit message  | Last commit date\nL173: --- | --- | --- | ---\nL174: 【69†.github】  | 【69†.github】  |    |\nL175: 【70†cutline】  | 【70†cutline】  |    |\nL176: 【71†docs】  | 【71†docs】  |    |\nL177: 【72†scripts】  | 【72†scripts】  |    |\nL178: 【73†static】  | 【73†static】  |    |\nL179: 【74†tests】  | 【74†tests】  |    |\nL180: 【75†.dockerignore】  | 【75†.dockerignore】  |    |\nL181: 【76†.env.example】  | 【76†.env.example】  |    |\nL182: 【77†.gitignore】  | 【77†.gitignore】  |    |\nL183: 【78†.mailmap】  | 【78†.mailmap】  |    |\nL248: The public web application uses an explicit, deterministic orchestration path so that provider calls, state transitions, and human approvals remain auditable. The ADK agent in `cutline/agents/claim_research/` packages the same research capability for Agent Engine.\nL249: \nL250: See 【88†Architecture】 for data flow, trust boundaries, and failure behavior.\nL251: ## Repository layout\nL252: \nL253:     `cutline/                       Application package\nL254:       agents/claim_research/       Google ADK agent and Parallel tool\nL255:       api.py                       HTTP routes, sessions, CSRF, headers, health checks\nL256:       domain.py                    Review fingerprints and state transitions\nL257:       providers.py                 Parallel Search and Vertex AI adapters\nL258:       store.py                     Memory and transactional Firestore stores\nL259:     static/                        Accessible responsive web interface\nL260:     scripts/                       GCP bootstrap, deployment, smoke, and repository checks\nL261:     tests/                         Domain, API, provider, security, and concurrency tests\nL262:     docs/                          Architecture, GCP setup, operations, and submission guide\nL263:     `\nL264: ## Local quickstart\nL265: \nL266: Requirements: Python 3.12–3.14.\nL267: \nL268:     python3 -m venv .venv\nL269:     source .venv/bin/activate\nL270:     python -m pip install --upgrade pip\nL271:     pip install -e '.[test]'\nL272:     cp .env.example .env\nL273:     set -a && source .env && set +a\nL274:     uvicorn cutline.main:app --reload --host 127.0.0.1 --port 8080\nL275: \nL276: Open http://127.0.0.1:8080. The defaults run in fixture mode and make no external provider calls.\nL277: ### Run validation\nL278: \nL279:     ruff check .\nL280:     ruff format --check .\nL281:     python -m unittest discover -s tests -v\nL282:     node --check static/app.js\nL283:     python scripts/validate_repo.py\nL284:     docker build -t cutline:local .\nL285: ## Live configuration\nL286: Variable  | Required in live mode  | Purpose\nL287: --- | --- | ---\nL288: `APP_MODE=live`  | Yes  | Enables the real provider path; no fixture fallback\nL289: `DATA_BACKEND=firestore`  | Yes  | Durable transactional state\nL290: `GOOGLE_CLOUD_PROJECT`  | Yes  | Billing and Vertex AI project\nL291: `GOOGLE_CLOUD_LOCATION`  | Yes  | Vertex AI API location; `global` is the default\nL292: `GOOGLE_GENAI_USE_VERTEXAI=TRUE`  | Yes for ADK  | Routes Google SDK calls to Vertex AI\nL293: `MODEL_ID`  | Yes  | Gemini model identifier; default `gemini-3.5-flash`\nL294: `PARALLEL_API_KEY`  | Yes  | Parallel Search credential, injected from Secret Manager\nL295: `SESSION_SECRET`  | Yes  | At least 32 random characters, injected from Secret Manager\nL296: `COOKIE_SECURE=[REDACTED_SECRET]  | Yes  | Secure browser session cookie\nL297: `ALLOWED_HOSTS`  | Yes  | Accepted service/custom domains\nL298: `PROJECT_TTL_HOURS`  | No  | Firestore retention window; default 72 hours\nL299: `PROVIDER_TIMEOUT_SECONDS`  | No  | Per-provider timeout; default 30 seconds\nL300: Do not put live credentials in `.env`, shell history, screenshots, repository files, build arguments, or client-side code. Cloud Run receives both secrets from Secret Manager.\nL301: ## Deploy\nL302: \nL303: Follow the complete 【89†Google Cloud setup】, or run the reviewed scripts in order:\nL304: \nL305:     export GOOGLE_CLOUD_PROJECT='your-project-id'\nL306:     export GOOGLE_CLOUD_LOCATION='us-central1'\nL307:     ./scripts/bootstrap_gcp.sh\nL308:     ./scripts/deploy_cloud_run.sh\nL309:     CREATE_AGENT_ENGINE=true ./scripts/deploy_agent_engine.sh\nL328:   * Fail-closed live configuration; no silent synthetic fallback.\nL329:   * HttpOnly, Secure, SameSite session cookie with HMAC authentication.\nL330:   * Derived CSRF token and same-origin mutation checks.\nL331:   * Strict request models, bounded bodies, cue lengths, result counts, and provider timeouts.\nL332:   * Optimistic concurrency with transactional Firestore writes.\nL333:   * Content Security Policy, HSTS, frame denial, MIME sniffing prevention, and restricted browser permissions.\nL334:   * No request bodies, evidence excerpts, or credentials in application logs.\nL335:   * Safe source-link protocol handling and output escaping.\nL336:   * CSV formula neutralization.\nL337:   * Quotation immutability and explicit confirmation before narration changes.\nL338:   * Human approval remains separate from provider assessment.\nL339:   * Firestore TTL and explicit project deletion.\nL340:   * Non-root, read-only application runtime apart from managed service clients.\nL341: See 【90†Operations】 for rollback, secret rotation, monitoring, and incident steps.\nL342: ## Production verification\nL343: \nL344: Local tests cover deterministic behavior and provider boundaries. A release owner must additionally verify the credentialed environment:\nL345:   1. Cloud Run readiness returns `200`.\nL346:   2. The UI says Live · Gemini + Parallel.\nL347:   3. The signature cue edit marks only dependent recipes stale.\nL348:   4. Research produces a receipt showing both providers were called.\nL349:   5. Displayed captures and source links correspond to the current provider response.\nL350:   6. A stale response cannot commit after another edit.\nL351:   7. Human approval and recipe signoff are required before export.\nL352:   8. Firestore persists state across a new Cloud Run instance.\nL353:   9. TTL and explicit deletion remove project records.\nL354:   10. Cloud Logging contains timing and error type only—no keys or content.\n"}]
